People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

And then I notice the crumbs, and I’m like, “What am I doing?” It’s been enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: After those nights when you eat a lot, do you ever make yourself vomit?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not on purpose. I’ve felt kind of gross or too full after, like why did I do that, but I don’t go make myself throw up. My sister used to get really nauseous from those diet shakes—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked. No, I haven’t been making myself vomit after.\n\nDoctor: When that happens at night, do you eat an unusually large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes it feels like a lot, at least compared to how I eat the rest of the time. Like, I’ll barely touch dinner for days, and then I’m in the kitchen late eating crackers and sweets after everyone’s asleep. I don’t know if it’s “unusually large” like you mean, but it feels kind of out of control in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Is your weight currently much lower than what’s typical for your height?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s much lower, exactly. I haven’t really been tracking it like that. What I’ve noticed more is the eating itself being all over the place—some weeks I barely eat dinner, and then other nights I’m in the kitchen after everyone’s asleep eating crackers and sweets. So… I’m not sure about my weight for my height, but the appetite thing feels off.\n\nDoctor: How much does your body shape or weight affect how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to measure that. I mean, I don’t love how I look sometimes, but I don’t know if my whole self-worth is tied to it. Some days I avoid mirrors, but then other times I’m more worried about whether I even ate normal that day. My niece is always talking about fitness apps and calories, and I try not to get pulled into that stuff… sorry, I’m drifting. I guess my weight or shape affects me some, but I don’t know how much.\n\nDoctor: When you eat like that at night, do you feel unable to stop once you’ve started?\n\nPatient: I think… sometimes, yeah, it feels that way. Not every time, but on those nights I’ll be standing there eating crackers or sweets after everyone’s gone to bed, and it’s like I meant to just have a little bit, but then I keep going. It’s strange because other weeks I barely even touch dinner, so it doesn’t feel like a normal pattern. That’s kind of what pushed me to come in, honestly—the eating being so all over the place.\n\nDoctor: During those late-night eating episodes, do you feel out of control while you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess that’s the part that bothers me. I’ll go in thinking I’m just grabbing a couple crackers or something sweet, and then I’m still standing there eating after everyone’s asleep. It’s not like I planned it. And then other weeks I barely touch dinner at all, so it feels… I don’t know, kind of out of my hands.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say I’m afraid of gaining weight exactly… it’s more that my eating has gotten weird and I notice it. Like some weeks I barely eat dinner, and then other nights I’m standing in the kitchen after everyone’s asleep eating crackers or sweets, and I sort of snap out of it like, “What am I doing?”

So… I guess weight is in the back of my mind, but that’s not really what pushed me to come in. It’s the back-and-forth with my appetite.

I mean, I don’t love how I look sometimes, but I don’t know if my whole self-worth is tied to it. Some days I avoid mirrors, but then other times I’m more worried about whether I even ate normal that day. My niece is always talking about fitness apps and calories, and I try not to get pulled into that stuff… sorry, I’m drifting. I guess my weight or shape affects me some, but I don’t know how much.\n\nDoctor: When you eat like that at night, do you feel unable to stop once you’ve started?\n\nPatient: I think… sometimes, yeah, it feels that way. Not every time, but on those nights I’ll be standing there eating crackers or sweets after everyone’s gone to bed, and it’s like I meant to just have a little bit, but then I keep going. It’s strange because other weeks I barely even touch dinner, so it doesn’t feel like a normal pattern. That’s kind of what pushed me to come in, honestly—the eating being so all over the place.\n\nDoctor: During those late-night eating episodes, do you feel out of control while you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess that’s the part that bothers me. I’ll go in thinking I’m just grabbing a couple crackers or something sweet, and then I’m still standing there eating after everyone’s asleep. It’s not like I planned it. And then other weeks I barely touch dinner at all, so it feels… I don’t know, kind of out of my hands. That up-and-down with eating is really what made me come in.\n\nDoctor: After those nights when you eat a lot, do you ever try to make up for it by using laxatives?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I’ve had laxatives in the house before, like normal medicine cabinet stuff, but I don’t really use them because of eating. After those nights I mostly just feel weird about it and tell myself I’ll do better the next day. No, I haven’t been trying to make up for it with laxatives.\n\nDoctor: After those nights when you eat a lot, do you ever exercise extra to try to make up for it?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think so. I think I’m awake? Like I remember being in the kitchen, and I remember the light feeling too bright sometimes. But it’s not like I sit there and make a plan, you know?\n\nSometimes I’ll realize I’ve opened a package and I’m eating, and then I feel kind of irritated with myself. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’m in the kitchen eating like that. That up-and-down with eating is really what made me come in.\n\nDoctor: After those nights when you eat a lot, do you ever try to make up for it by using laxatives?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I’ve had laxatives in the house before, like normal medicine cabinet stuff, but I don’t really use them because of eating. After those nights I mostly just feel weird about it and tell myself I’ll do better the next day. My sister used to keep all kinds of teas and cleanses around, and I remember thinking that seemed like a whole project, but—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I haven’t been trying to make up for it with laxatives.\n\nDoctor: After those nights when you eat a lot, do you ever exercise extra to try to make up for it?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think so. I mean, I sometimes tell myself I should go for a walk or “get back on track,” but I don’t usually do some big workout because I ate at night.\n\nI used to have this exercise DVD—well, not used to, I still have it somewhere—and I’d think about doing it in the morning, but then the morning is its own mess with coffee and the phone and everything. So… no, I haven’t really noticed myself exercising extra to make up for it.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes happen after you wake up from sleep during the night?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah, it’s like I’ll be asleep and then I’m up in the kitchen, but other times I’m just still awake too late. That’s kind of the thing that finally made me come in—my eating feels all over the place. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’m standing there after everyone’s gone to bed eating crackers or sweets, like I didn’t really mean for it to turn into that.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating after waking in the night, are you fully awake and aware of what you’re doing?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. I think I’m awake? Like I remember being in the kitchen, and I remember the light feeling too bright sometimes. But it’s not like I sit there and make a plan, you know?\n\nSometimes I’ll realize I’ve opened a package and I’m eating, and then I feel kind of irritated with myself. My mom used to say you shouldn’t eat standing up because it “doesn’t count,” which is a funny thing to remember in the middle of the night, but… I guess I’m aware, just kind of foggy.\n\nDoctor: When you eat during those nighttime episodes, are you fully awake?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think I’m awake enough to know I’m eating, but the bigger thing is my eating’s just been all over the place. That’s kind of what finally pushed me to come in.\n\nSome weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’m just standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets. It’s not like a nice snack on a plate, it’s just… there I am. So awake, yes, I guess, but it feels kind of automatic.\n\nDoctor: When these nighttime eating episodes happen, are you fully awake?\n\nPatient: I think so, yeah… awake enough, anyway. But honestly what’s been bothering me more is how weird my eating has gotten. Like some weeks I barely even touch dinner, and then other nights I’m in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets standing there like I didn’t even decide to do it. It just feels all over the place.\n\nDoctor: What is your current height and weight?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t actually know my exact weight right now. My height’s the same, obviously, but I haven’t been checking the scale much.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is more that my eating’s been all over the place—like some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’m in the kitchen after everyone’s asleep eating crackers or sweets standing up. So I wouldn’t be surprised if my weight’s changed, but I don’t have the number in my head.\n\nDoctor: After those nights when you eat a lot, do you intentionally eat much less the next day to make up for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like a planned “make up for it” thing, no. It’s not like I’m making a full meal usually… more like I’ll be standing there after everyone’s gone to bed eating crackers or sweets, and then I kind of realize I’ve been doing it for a while.\n\nThe bigger thing is just how uneven it’s gotten. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’m in the kitchen eating like that. It’s that back-and-forth that finally made me think, okay, something’s off.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m afraid of gaining weight exactly… it’s more that my eating has gotten weird and I notice it. Like some weeks I barely eat dinner, and then other nights I’m standing in the kitchen after everyone’s asleep eating crackers or sweets, and I sort of snap out of it like, “What am I doing?”\n\nSo… I guess weight is in the back of my mind, but that’s not really what pushed me to come in.
